# Unfilter digest 04 — baby and toddler sleep

Prepared 2026-11-16 from the assigned re-read share. Destination: the next rewrite of `synthesis/findings.md`; this digest does not edit that file.

| Voice file | Quote records read in full | Ledger summary read |
|---|---:|---|
| `voice/reddit-ur04.jsonl` | 337 | `sources/reddit-reading-ur04.md` — “What these posts add” |
| `voice/reddit-ur14.jsonl` | 413 | `sources/reddit-reading-ur14.md` — “What these posts add” |
| `voice/reddit-ur18.jsonl` | 371 | `sources/reddit-reading-ur18.md` — “What these posts add” |
| **Total** | **1,121** | **All three summaries** |

These are counts of quote records, never people. The posts were selected because they had previously been excluded; this share cannot establish prevalence, rates, typicality or method rankings. Every quote field is reproduced in full below, including fragments, reported medication use, harm, abuse, loss, suicidal thoughts and conflicting safety beliefs. The original IDs retain provenance in the three voice files. The ledgers supply context where a field alone is incomplete. No network or git was used.

The current findings already restore medical uncertainty, dangerous exhaustion, coercion and mixed experiences of cosleeping. Much of this share confirms those revisions. The most consequential changes are the opening’s blanket rejection of philosophy, the father segment’s wife-centred emphasis, sleep need described as falling in one direction, the universal price advantage over people, the claimed absence of stopping guidance, and causal accounts missing a simultaneous intervention. Detailed comparisons follow each pattern statement.

## Patterns and changes to the current findings

### 1. Parents ask about the kind of care they should give as well as the steps that might produce sleep.

**Current section:** §1; §§5.5, 6.2, 6.4 and 10.

**Relationship:** Contradicts the absolute opening claim; extends the values requirement.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> She does not want a philosophy.

**What to change or retain:** Replace this absolute with a split: some want short instructions, some want evidence compatible with attachment or evolutionary parenting, and some want both. The cortisol premise and fears of giving up are the speakers’ beliefs. A response-to-crying preference cannot capture all of these questions.

**Evidence:** 33 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 2. Judgment changes what parents disclose, whom they consult and whether they trust sleep content.

**Current section:** §§3, 6.3, 7 and 11; §17.5 provenance.

**Relationship:** Confirms judgment from both camps and extends it to clinical nondisclosure and product trust.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> Parents are also trying to escape conflicting certainty and find practical help without being condemned.

**What to change or retain:** Add the mother who avoids discussing bedsharing with a health professional because it is taboo, then asks strangers for a floor-bed setup. Keep family pressure, warnings that every improvement will deteriorate, reactions to sleep-training posts, and distrust of an app after its formula brand’s handling of a botulism outbreak distinct. The outbreak handling is the speaker’s allegation; no external check was performed.

**Evidence:** 14 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 3. Prematurity, surgery, feeding treatment and a previous baby’s death can remove the usual sleep options.

**Current section:** §3 medical uncertainty; §§6.2, 6.3, 6.4, 9.6 and 10.

**Relationship:** Extends the existing conclusion that health and feeding shape fit.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> Some people want responsive clinical or emotional support, some want to retain cosleeping, and some cannot tolerate either that arrangement or crying methods.

**What to change or retain:** Add explicit decision constraints: monitored NICU tummy sleep followed by difficult back sleep at home; rolling that removes the planned swaddle; prematurity-related refusal of bedsharing; a family limiting crying after open-heart surgery; recovery from cranial reconstruction in a single parent’s open-plan home; and low intake under feeding therapy with substantial night calories. Preserve the parent who lost a first baby after surgery and explicitly distinguishes that death from SIDS. The adoptive mother’s paediatrician/therapist involvement and the ICU-to-crib hypothesis are care histories, not proven causes.

**Evidence:** 24 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 4. Parents describe waking around pain, reflux, itching, stools, illness and treatment uncertainty.

**Current section:** §3; §§4.1, 4.5, 6.2, 8 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms medical uncertainty and extends its mechanisms, treatment sequences and access limits.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> Parents report contradictory feeding and room advice, a referral refused, tests, medication and supplements that helped, failed or stopped helping, plus questions about whether a medical need is being mistaken for a habit.

**What to change or retain:** Add the feed–explosive-nappy–alertness cycle; itching despite Zyrtec and three days of relief after a steroid; teething presumed before strep was identified; recurrent ear infections and tubes questions; constipation, snoring/tonsils, allergy investigations and negative tests without an answer. Keep medication names, doses, failures and parental actions intact, including adult Pepto-Bismol given while exhausted, melatonin/magnesium/Benadryl experiments, and a doctor’s reported Benadryl suggestion. These are accounts of actions and claimed effects, not medical instructions or established causation. Budget cuts delaying assessment belong with access, while the parent’s melatonin claim remains attributed.

**Evidence:** 139 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 5. Night feeds combine nutrition, comfort, bodily aversion, pregnancy, work and dental questions.

**Current section:** §§3, 6.1, 6.2, 9.1, 9.3 and 10; feeding-related branches in §17.2.

**Relationship:** Extends the distinction between a feeding association and an actual feeding constraint.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> Health, feeding, physical capacity, mental state, available help, living space and the cost of professional assessment all appear in the restored words (§§3–6, 8–9).

**What to change or retain:** Distinguish parents deliberately retaining calories for a small baby, infants too sleepy or averse to finish feeds, bottle refusal preventing a night handover, and parents trying to stop because nursing makes their skin crawl, nipples are bleeding or pregnancy makes feeding painful. Preserve positive attachment to nursing alongside wanting to stop. A fixed two-bottle routine due to bottle aversion, a clinician’s milk-reduction request, toothbrushing after a cough-triggered night feed, watering down feeds and a partner-led weaning success require different descriptions. Night-weaning success and peaceful child-led readiness also remain visible.

**Evidence:** 63 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 6. A caregiver’s pain or disability can make a proposed sleep arrangement physically impossible.

**Current section:** §9.1 body and health; §§4.6, 6.2, 9.6 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms physical capacity as a fit constraint and extends it to equipment access and cot conversion.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> a caregiver may be physically unable to sustain rocking, holding or a walking-based solution.

**What to change or retain:** Add the disabled parent climbing into a cot two to five times a night, back pain driving a cot-to-bed decision, pelvic-floor pain preventing babywearing, lifting a heavy baby out of a low sleep space, and POTS flares linked by the speaker to poor sleep. Bedsharing can provide child sleep while producing stiffness, c-curl back injury or fear in the parent. The rocking-chair purchase question is partly an ergonomics request. Do not collapse these into low motivation or failure to persist.

**Evidence:** 19 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 7. Parents’ risk decisions depend on the baby’s movement, the sleep surface and what they can observe.

**Current section:** §6.4; §§4.6, 6.1, 9.6 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms fear of harm and extends it to concrete nighttime decisions.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> The full words connect breathing checks, fear of infant death, guilt about both sleep arrangements, fear of lasting harm and dread that the next regression will repeat an earlier collapse.

**What to change or retain:** Retain the precise situations: a baby wedged or rolling in a crib, a parent repeatedly repositioning, concern about a blanket or a mattress arrangement, uncertainty about a reused family crib, and parents who cannot accept bedsharing. Some reinterpret calm sleep as possible illness, medication effects or a failure to hear crying. Record the decisions and beliefs without adopting the parents’ safety claims as verified rules. Breathing-monitor specifics are separated below; accidents and injuries also have their own patterns.

**Evidence:** 50 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 8. Monitoring can reassure, create repeated alerts, or leave parents without a response plan.

**Current section:** §4.3 tracking/monitoring; §§6.1, 6.4 and 8.

**Relationship:** Extends the monitor-anxiety conclusion with contrary reassurance and practical gaps.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> Monitors can misclassify waking or generate repeated alerts

**What to change or retain:** Keep the Owlet user who explicitly prefers false alarms to uncertainty and asks what to do during an eleven-minute journey to emergency care. Add the purchased radar monitor losing a rolling baby at 3am, the narrow camera view that prompts hourly checks, and the toddler nearing an age limit displayed by an app whose parent wants a replacement breathing monitor. A bedsharing parent worries that a sleep sack muffles the kicks that would alert her to a duvet over the baby. These device claims and safety beliefs are the speakers’ reports.

**Evidence:** 11 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

<a id="sbu14-0171"></a>
**[sbu14-0171]**

> (7) This is more of a public opinion recommendation — using a pulse/ox monitor. While monitors like the Owlet have not been shown to reduce or stop SIDS, they can possibly alert you to intervene if baby’s pulse or oxygen levels become poor. There currently isn’t any research that shows that if SIDS begins, parents can intervene and stop it, but this doesn’t mean it can’t either, there’s just no definitive proof and there’s no way to ethically study this. HOWEVER, occasionally the Owlet has been used as a safety net to start or continue sleeping practices that are not recommended, so please keep that in mind. Additionally, some parents report feeling more anxious with the use of these kind of monitors. I mention these because of their rise in popularity and are frequently discussed in comment sections regarding SIDS and likely to pop up in a comment on this post. My pediatrician said they are “complementary” to the rest of the recommendations.

<a id="sbu14-0172"></a>
**[sbu14-0172]**

> My goal of writing all of this out is to provide both education and some comfort as many of us face the scary reality that yes, SIDS does exist, but we are not powerless in it. My prayer is that all of our precious and beloved babies grow up happy and healthy, and that their parents are able to get some sleep at night despite the very natural anxiety parenthood brings.

### 9. Exhaustion produces actual falls and unsafe dozing as well as fear of them.

**Current section:** §9.1 exhaustion changes care; §§4.6, 5.1 and 6.4.

**Relationship:** Confirms dangerous exhaustion and extends it with incident sequences and equipment complaints.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> These are concrete triggers for asking for change: falling asleep while holding the baby, finding the baby face down in a pillow, a near road accident and a demanding clinical shift.

**What to change or retain:** Add the baby dropped during feeding, the six-day-old whose parent wakes to a thud after unintentional sleep, repeated bassinet falls, the report of a head hitting a bed frame, and the father who nodded off on a public bench and separately left a sleeping toddler in a car to collect a dog. Preserve the Newton travel-crib account of a child under the mattress and the parent’s search in the dark. A parent stopping prescribed dihydrocodeine after a fall is a reported personal action, not a recommended trade-off. Keep event reports separate from verified product defects.

**Evidence:** 20 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 10. Bedsharing can provide rest, shorten soothing, stop helping, or leave parent and baby awake.

**Current section:** §5.5 cosleeping; §§2, 6.2, 6.3, 9.5 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms the revised mixed-outcome conclusion and extends the range of goals and transitions.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> Some value the closeness, some want to keep it while changing feeds or naps, some cannot sleep that way, and some reject both cosleeping and crying methods.

**What to change or retain:** Keep valued snuggles and peaceful chest sleep, success confined to the second half of the night, waking that continues beside a parent, active babies who kick or play, and children who need to be held rather than merely beside someone. Some want a crib transition with night feeds retained; some want their bed or evening back; some simply need a less exhausting version of the arrangement. Bedsharing failure can itself become an objection to trying training. Pain and housing mechanisms are detailed in other patterns. No one outcome establishes a ranking of methods or the safety of a setup.

**Evidence:** 208 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 11. Reported sleep needs vary and can increase; rigid nap caps can make daytime life worse.

**Current section:** §4.4; §§6.2, 17.2, 17.6 and 17.9.

**Relationship:** Contradicts the one-direction assumption in the longer-tier interpretation; confirms individual variation.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> dated re-checks of the schedule as sleep need falls

**What to change or retain:** Change this to re-checks as sleep needs and circumstances change. One parent explicitly reports increased need at twelve months. Another reports prolonged misery under caps and two better nights after allowing catch-up sleep; retain the short follow-up and the same quote’s stated intention to resume caps. Other parents describe genuinely low needs, very early nap dropping, or worse nights with less awake time. A successful low-sleep schedule does not justify automatically cutting sleep for another baby. Asking whether a child needs more sleep while every sleep is capped is a distinct unresolved measurement problem.

**Evidence:** 49 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

<a id="sbu14-0324"></a>
**[sbu14-0324]**

> Baby (12m) was on a schedule that was really working for her (3.25/3.75/4). We had been upping her wake time slowly and it was working great! But over the past month or so, her sleep needs appear to have increased. She has trouble handling her full wake windows now (and she’s not just bored… I can tell the difference with how she acts during her bedtime routine). Lately, we’ve been doing more like 3/3.5/3.75. I feel like an additional 45 minutes of sleep per day is quite a lot? And I’m still worried I’m depriving her of sleep because I’m usually having to wake her up.

<a id="sbu14-0358"></a>
**[sbu14-0358]**

> Which brings us to now. Nap is capped at 1.5 hours. Nights are 10 hours (and closer to 9.5 now). He is TIRED! He is cranky and irritable, rarely wakes up happy anymore since I started this strict schedule. If he has a MOTN waking especially and still wakes up before 7, its a nightmare. When I wake him up from his naps, its such a bad meltdown that sometimes it takes him almost an hour to snap out of it. I am talking an hour of absolute meltdowns with crying, screaming, and nothing works to soothe him. And to top it all off, he is just miserable all day.

If he starts waking up again, I am definitely capping it at 1 hour.

### 12. A recovery or relapse often follows several simultaneous changes, leaving the cause unresolved.

**Current section:** §2; §§4.4, 16.2, 17.2 and 17.9.

**Relationship:** Confirms recurring setbacks and extends the restored caution about causal claims.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> These selected accounts do not establish causes or validate a treatment protocol

**What to change or retain:** Correct any red-light success account to include simethicone given that night and the mother’s inability to separate the effects. Add the parent’s developmental/separation-anxiety explanation alongside the previously attributed schedule recovery. Bedtime shifts plus retraining, concurrent analgesia, altered feeds, illness and new movement skills also make isolated success claims unreliable. Keep one-night wins, two-night wins, repeated setbacks, and different results on nearly identical schedules at their stated follow-up lengths. These records do not establish a universal restart interval or the causal distinction implied by the two-clocks shorthand.

**Evidence:** 58 quote records assigned to this pattern, plus 1 cross-pattern quotations. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 13. Independent sleep onset, nap consolidation, night continuity and daytime wellbeing are separate outcomes.

**Current section:** §§2, 4.4, 6.1, 9.1 and 17.2.

**Relationship:** Extends the outcome definitions and qualifies a simple schedule-fix versus training divide.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> formal training holds for weeks to years and then breaks on one trigger.

**What to change or retain:** Add the baby who falls asleep independently with little crying but keeps waking; easy crib naps beside major waking-hour distress; a month of nap training with rescues that the parent says did not undo progress; and spontaneous consolidation with no new intervention. Preserve quick training successes and a rare check-in that calmed rather than escalated. A child sleeping well still does not establish usable rest or creative capacity for the parent. The duration of formal training is variable, and success in one outcome should not be substituted for another.

**Evidence:** 25 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

I tried not to worry about “backsliding” and prioritized getting him to sleep rather than forcing him to cry longer (of course I was worried during this time that anything I did to help him to sleep would mess up the independent sleep progress, but  ultimately it didn’t)

### 14. Parents seek response and stopping rules, and some already report being given one.

**Current section:** §4.5; §§6.1, 17.6 and 17.9.

**Relationship:** Confirms the need for rules; qualifies the absolute claim that nobody answers it.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> A plan that gives a stop rule, a vomiting rule and a partner script answers a question nobody else in the corpus answers.

**What to change or retain:** Qualify the claim of absence: a parent reports a paediatrician recommending at least an hour of crying but not much longer, and says this limit made starting tolerable. Another successful-training account advises a cuddle and restarting when the child is hysterical. These do not provide all three branches named in the current sentence, but they contradict a blanket absence of supplied stopping guidance. Other records ask whether to comfort, feed or continue. Reported advice is not a validated threshold.

**Evidence:** 16 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 15. Vomiting, breathing distress, falls and lost teeth make stopping an account of events rather than weak commitment.

**Current section:** §4.5; §§6.4, 9.4, 17.6 and 17.9.

**Relationship:** Confirms the restored stopping conclusion and extends it with concrete injuries and sequences.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> lost voices, choking, breath holding, vomiting, a child getting hurt in the cot and a parent who cannot tolerate the crying.

**What to change or retain:** Add the two-year-old losing two teeth during nap protest, dental x-rays, antibiotics and fear of using the crib again. Keep repeated head impacts when standing, self-injury on a door/dresser/trim, vomiting across consultant-led approaches, breath-holding with prior passing out, choking/laboured-breathing accounts, and recurring hoarseness. The speakers’ descriptions and interpretations remain intact; no inferred mechanism, diagnosis or endorsement of pushing through is added.

**Evidence:** 30 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 16. Some settling attempts involve holding the child’s body still while the child resists.

**Current section:** §4.5; §§4.1, 6.4 and 9.4.

> A stop is not simply failure to persist.

Preserve the parent’s own child-abuse comparison. Related double-swaddling and holding legs down remain in the sleepwear pattern. These are reported actions, not a recommended settling procedure.

### 22. Housing, siblings, pets, work and heat determine which sleep changes a household can attempt.

**Current section:** §9.6; §§5.1, 5.3, 6.2, 8 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms household constraints and extends their specific mechanisms.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> The formerly marked lines make constraints more exact: unaffordable separate rooms, only one room with air conditioning, waking a twin or sibling, night calls and lack of alternative housing.

**What to change or retain:** Add mandatory bedsharing after separation in someone else’s unbabyproofable home, an iPad bought partly to protect a sibling’s naps, dogs waking a child by striking a wall or barking at 5am, and shift work/travel/grandparent care that defeats fixed nap times. Heat can make a trip attractive despite sleep disruption. Coordinating two children can leave three hours of alternating screaming or remove both parents’ evenings. The frequency and sound of disruption matter alongside wake counts.

**Evidence:** 29 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 23. Toddler nights include confinement, punishment, fear, night terrors and touch that makes distress worse.

**Current section:** §9.4; §§4.5, 5.1, 6.1 and 6.4.

**Relationship:** Extends the brief toddler portrait into distinct control, comfort and safety decisions.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> They describe bedtime battles, climbing out of the cot, a parent stuck in the room, and crying methods that feel wrong at this age

**What to change or retain:** Add locking a 2.5-year-old’s door and asking whether it is abuse; threatening/removing a nightlight and questioning fear as motivation; a parent who changes their view of room containment because of fire; and uncertainty about when to intervene during reported night terrors to prevent a fall. Keep the newly adopted twenty-two-month-old’s thrashing, hitting and rejection of comforting touch with the paediatric/therapist context in the clinical pattern; no trauma explanation is proven. Other struggles involve inconsistent book boundaries, pacifier loss, curtains moved by air conditioning, school avoidance and four months of silent returns.

**Evidence:** 42 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 24. Night sharing depends on feeding, trust, caregiver preference and each adult’s capacity to settle.

**Current section:** §§5.1, 5.2, 9.2, 9.6 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms the varied night split and extends why a willing handover can still fail.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> Being held or sleeping beside a parent can work for the baby while leaving neither caregiver enough rest.

**What to change or retain:** Separate successful shifts and practical teamwork from handovers defeated by bottle refusal, a baby who rejects one parent, one adult’s mental health or a job requiring alertness. One mother cannot rest because she listens, worries and takes over despite no prior incident of her husband falling asleep; another sleeps in the guest room while her husband has the baby because every sound wakes her. Keep the genuinely intoxicated and dozing partner separate from anticipated harm. Both adults being in bed, offering help and obtaining sleep are different facts.

**Evidence:** 52 quote records assigned to this pattern. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 25. Some requests for sleep relief occur within threats, violence, restricted help or financial dependence.

**Current section:** §5.1; §§6.3, 6.4, 9.1 and 9.6.

**Relationship:** Confirms the restored distinction between coercion and ordinary method disagreement; extends the incidents.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> These are different from two willing caregivers needing the same script.

**What to change or retain:** Name what is reported: throwing a baby hard onto a bed and threatening to throw him against a wall; asking for abortion and threatening not to return home after a pregnant mother asks for an hour’s sleep; making help conditional on agreement; shoving during a conflict about taking the baby; withholding daycare payment; and financial/housing dependence that makes leaving feel impossible. A shared script is not evidenced as a solution to these conditions. Preserve direct statements, including admissions of control and anger issues, without inferring unreported events.

### 26. Fathers describe their own panic, burnout, treatment fears, anger and harm-related events.

**Current section:** §9.2; §§5.1, 9.1 and 10.

The father accused of an aggressive diaper change explicitly denies that interpretation. Expectations to power through and a mother’s account that the father functions worse after broken sleep also belong here.

### 27. Parents describe hopelessness, rage, regret and lost functioning that waking counts cannot explain.

**Current section:** §9.1; §§1, 6.4 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms the restored care-constraint conclusion and extends its limits and desired outcomes.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> needing tolerable care, sleep and support

**What to change or retain:** Preserve explicit suicidal thoughts, self-hitting, rage and intrusive harm thoughts, resentment, loss of identity, body effects, and the wish to remain able to care. Include the father who enjoys low-stakes crying in the values evidence as contrary affect, and parents who say sleep loss itself is not the immediate trigger. A well-sleeping baby can coexist with severe anxiety or rage; another mother says she needs practical help rather than a depression label. A mother wishes her disruptive dog would die and says she would not act on the intrusive thoughts. Relief after bedtime can end in staring at a wall without capacity for chores or creative work. These accounts do not establish a diagnosis or a baby-plan treatment effect.

**Evidence:** 120 quote records assigned to this pattern, plus 1 cross-pattern quotations. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

### 28. An opportunity to sleep can coexist with insomnia, vigilance, hallucinations and no usable rest.

**Current section:** §9.1 own sleep/mental state; §§4.3, 5.1 and 10.

**Relationship:** Confirms the existing limit on the get-yourself-back promise and extends the mechanism.

**Current conclusion, verbatim:**

> It does not support promising that the parent's own sleep will come back when the baby's does

**What to change or retain:** Add the parent who receives five uninterrupted hours and sleeps only two, cannot take sleeping pills because of drowsy care, and reports hallucinations. Another cannot sleep even when her husband offers the whole night and describes a shadow figure threatening to take her baby; she credits medication, therapy, husband and village with recovery. In a mother-and-baby unit, repeated choking checks prevent rest. Other accounts anticipate the next wake as a two-hour time bomb or cannot sleep despite a generally easy child. Child sleep, offered respite, sleep obtained, free time and capacity to use it need separate outcome language.

**Evidence:** 16 quote records assigned to this pattern, plus 1 cross-pattern quotations. Distinctive passages come first; all remaining assigned fields follow in ID order. All support is reproduced, so there are no additional support-only IDs for this pattern.

<a id="sbu04-0087"></a>
**[sbu04-0087]**

> Due to my lack of sleep because I have a 6 month old who absolutely hates sleeping in his crib and wants to be held all night I now have awful insomnia. So on top of being beyond exhausted to the point where I start to hallucinate when I attempt to fall asleep I can’t? Isn’t that just GREAT. Last night my husband took our son and said go sleep for 5 hours uninterrupted I was so beyond happy to finally get rest. Guess how many hours of the 5 I actually slept? 2! I was tossing and turning over and over for hours and hours. What can I do? I’m so tired. I can’t take sleeping pills because I don’t want to be too drowsy when caring for my son.

## Outliers and counter-examples

These are separate reported cases. They qualify the patterns above and belong in the rewrite alongside failures, fear and exhaustion. Quotation repeats here do not add new quote records to the coverage count.

### 1. Content contact sleep and bedsharing are real outcomes.

Cuts against treating all contact sleepers as wanting independent sleep or assuming shared sleep fails.

**[sbu18-0095]**

> He does sleep on my chest (happy cosleeper guildlines) and only wakes slightly if I need to get up and pee or if he needs a change, which is usually only twice a night. He doesn't fully wake though and when he's hungry he just lightly roots and finds me and feeds himself back to sleep. The routine is usually pretty peaceful.

**[sbu18-0184]**

> If he is in our bed he sleeps through the night no issue. Doesn’t  wake even once 7pm-7am.  So I know this has to be about soothing and the crib.

**[sbu14-0205]**

> And honestly, we discovered something wonderful. Side lying breastfeeding in bed works incredibly well for us at night. He nurses, falls asleep, and the night itself actually goes pretty smoothly this way.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu18-0001](#sbu18-0001) [sbu18-0160](#sbu18-0160) [sbu04-0147](#sbu04-0147).

### 2. A refusal to train can coexist with a tolerable night and a modest goal.

Cuts against interpreting every night feed or sleep association as a problem the parent wants removed.

**[sbu04-0005]**

> However he sleeps pretty decently at night, only 2 wake ups to nurse. I am not trying to cut those down as my baby is little (1 percenter so I don’t mind the extra calories)

**[sbu18-0259]**

> I’m not expecting baby to sleep through the night, he is exclusively breastfed and I’m happy to do up to 3 night wakes honestly! I just wish he would sleep independently in his own space for safety and sanity reasons.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu04-0268](#sbu04-0268) [sbu14-0270](#sbu14-0270) [sbu14-0377](#sbu14-0377) [sbu18-0315](#sbu18-0315).

### 3. Training can work quickly, and check-ins can calm the child.

Counters a failure-only account of training, while preserving the stated follow-up and the distinction between bedtime and the full night.

**[sbu04-0198]**

> First night (3/5/10 minute Ferber check-ins) he was out before the 10 minute check-in. Second night (5/10/12) he was out before the first check-in, and now he pretty much falls asleep within five minutes of me laying him down at bedtime, with pretty minimal crying (and SO MUCH LESS than was happening before).

**[sbu14-0373]**

> Night 1 - 25 minutes of screaming/crying, but the check ins calmed him down (huge surprise)  
> No first wake up   
> 20 minutes of crying after a dream feed

**[sbu14-0374]**

> Night 5 - Asleep in 2 minutes  
> No first wake up   
> 5 minutes of crying at 4am, back to sleep

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu04-0314](#sbu04-0314) [sbu18-0319](#sbu18-0319) [sbu18-0323](#sbu18-0323).

### 4. A parent reports sustained CIO success and an actual supplied crying limit.

Counters an inference that no clinician supplies a bound or that the selected distress accounts invalidate all training successes.

**[sbu18-0285]**

*Record context identifies the reported advice as the paediatrician’s crying limit.*

> She also recommended letting her cry for at least an hour but not much longer than that and having that limit really helped us feel okay about giving it a try.

**[sbu18-0286]**

> It couldn't be going better! She's almost 8 months now. Sleeps independently at night and puts herself down for all naps too (it's a miracle, I thought naps would never happen). Any crying is rare  but if she does cry it's for under a minute. Normally she babbles, squeals, or rolls around for about 2 minutes and then is out like a light!

### 5. Helping during nap training did not inevitably erase progress.

Qualifies a rigid consistency rule and the expectation of improvement within only a few nights.

**[sbu14-0349]**

> It took another \~2 weeks to become consistent. So overall took about a month of “nap training.” During these 2ish weeks, sometimes he’d do it on his own, sometimes I’d have to bounce him fully to sleep, sometimes I could just pat his butt until he fell asleep - it was kind of all over the place but we kept the pre nap routine and the 15 mins “fuss it out” consistent every day, and eventually it just…started working. I tried not to worry about “backsliding” and prioritized getting him to sleep rather than forcing him to cry longer (of course I was worried during this time that anything I did to help him to sleep would mess up the independent sleep progress, but  ultimately it didn’t)

**[sbu14-0350]**

> I did nothing different, just one day to the next he started connecting sleep cycles and started napping 1-1.5 hours at a time.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu18-0171](#sbu18-0171).

### 6. A free human-supported programme reports success.

Counters a universal paid-plan price advantage over people; the pre-intervention distress is preserved with the outcome in the human-help pattern.

**[sbu04-0258]**

> At 6 months old we finally caved and we attended a 4 night stay at a sleep school (free council initiative).
> 
> By night 3, our daughter was sleeping 11 hours overnight in her crib and not waking at all, and sleeping in her crib for every. single. nap. 
> 
> We did not do extinction, they taught us a method they called in-and-out which I’m happy to explain in more detail in the comments.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu04-0257](#sbu04-0257).

### 7. Development, an unplanned room move and readiness can accompany improvement.

Counters attributing every improvement to a method or schedule intervention.

**[sbu04-0252]**

> I think it was a developmental leap and a bout of separation anxiety because she was also making stranger with my family. She’s since gotten over that and her sleep is back to normal.

**[sbu14-0347]**

> Two days ago, my husband and I had work done to our master bathroom that is connected to our bedroom where our baby would sleep in the SNOO right next to me. Well, after the work was done there was a strong fume smell that lingered and I did not want my 11 week old to have to smell it so I let him sleep in his own room (crib). I wasn’t expected it to go well but he slept AWESOME.

**[sbu18-0022]**

> It's helpful to remember weaning in light of this conversation, because with both the night weaning and the full weaning, I remember feeling like it was unfathomable that he would ever be ready to stop. But both times, he suddenly made it clear he was ready and then each process was relatively peaceful.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu14-0350](#sbu14-0350) [sbu18-0297](#sbu18-0297).

### 8. Low needs and unusually early nap dropping also occur in the accounts.

Balances the increased-need and cap-distress evidence; a variable-needs conclusion must retain both directions.

**[sbu18-0367]**

> She stopped taking regular naps shortly after turning one, but rewarded us with 11-13 hours of solid sleep every night and her pediatrician said all was fine.

**[sbu14-0266]**

> For context, my sleep trained baby is nearly 12m and very low sleep needs. We struggled massively till we realised this at around 7/8 months and cut sleep drastically, then finally everything fell into place. We're now down to around 10hrs sleep per day and already having to cap the 1 nap at 30mins to protect his night sleep. We know that he could easily drop sleep needs further before he's ready to drop the nap.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu18-0262](#sbu18-0262) [sbu04-0329](#sbu04-0329).

### 9. Good child sleep can coexist with severe parental distress or lost capacity.

Counters a causal promise that resolving baby sleep restores the parent’s functioning.

**[sbu14-0052]**

> My baby even sleeps pretty well! So I’m not even that sleep deprived??? And she’s not a huge crier and is very chill.

**[sbu14-0387]**

> I can't even complain about sleep: our kid has always slept well at night. I feel like I've been doing this on easy mode.

**[sbu14-0388]**

> Friends, the creative work has not gone well. I'm exhausted by the time I sit at my desk. My brain is full of details about taking care of the toddler and managing our lives. It's very hard to care about writing. I haven't gotten much done in a long time.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu04-0144](#sbu04-0144) [sbu14-0051](#sbu14-0051) [sbu14-0411](#sbu14-0411) [sbu18-0061](#sbu18-0061).

### 10. Some parents enjoy parts of crying or find a different trigger more upsetting than sleep loss.

Cuts against treating crying as uniformly unbearable or waking counts as a complete measure of distress.

**[sbu04-0189]**

> But if it's low stakes? Like the meltdown is "you're trying to put me to sleep but I don't wanna sleep" or "you took away something from me that I want to play with" or "you're trying to give me a bath but I'm not interested" or "I'm so tired and exhausted that I can't deal with anything"? Then I just absolutely love the crying, the tears, the sobbing and heaving, everything. I have to hold back from smiling or laughing because I assume that's not a nice reaction for her to see, and I have to pretend to be concerned.

**[sbu04-0172]**

> The lack of sleep isn’t really bugging me. The spilled milk on top of the screaming was what did me in. Hoping tonight goes smoother!

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu04-0190](#sbu04-0190) [sbu18-0187](#sbu18-0187).

### 11. A monitor can explicitly reduce anxiety.

Balances alert fatigue, compulsive checks and the conclusion that monitoring can increase distress.

**[sbu04-0091]**

> I get that it can increase anxiety for some parents, but for me it’s the opposite. I’d rather have an alarm that turns out to be a false alarm than wake up to find my baby unresponsive. The Owlet doesn’t replace safe sleep or medical care, but it gives me extra peace of mind. Every parent has a different comfort level.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu04-0289](#sbu04-0289).

### 12. Night weaning and crib transitions can succeed.

Balances painful weaning, persistent night calories and failed shared-bed exits; these are separate reported cases.

**[sbu14-0348]**

> I kept this feed for a while bc I was having complicated feelings about stopping breastfeeding, but when he was about 5.5 months I weaned the same way we did bottle - drop a min per side per night, until we got to about 4 mins total, then stopped offering the feed. The night I decided to stop offering the feed, I tried to sooth baby myself but in hindsight this was a big mistake bc he just wanted the boob and would NOT settle, so the next few nights my husband went in to do the soothing in place of feeding. After about 3 rough nights of this, he just stopped waking at that time, and started sleeping til about 530/6, which felt truly magical.

**[sbu18-0297]**

> At 14/15 months we transitioned her back into her cot and this went really well. She slept in her cot in my room until about 17 months and got to the point she was sleeping all night and would fall asleep without comfort from me.

Further counter-evidence, reproduced in full under the patterns: [sbu04-0137](#sbu04-0137) [sbu18-0022](#sbu18-0022).

## What fits nowhere

The present headings are broad enough to hold the sleep-related evidence. These subjects have no dedicated section, or need a provenance home because they cannot support a standalone conclusion. No field is discarded for that reason.

### 1. No standalone section for the hospital-to-home change in sleep position.

The nearest home is §3, but it deserves a named subtopic connecting monitored NICU tummy sleep, back sleep at home and movement that removes swaddling as an option.

Full passages: [sbu18-0044](#sbu18-0044) [sbu18-0045](#sbu18-0045) [sbu18-0046](#sbu18-0046).

### 2. No adoptive-parent or newly placed toddler section.

The current toddler and responsive-parent headings do not describe a newly adopted twenty-two-month-old who rejects comforting touch, becomes more dysregulated with contact, and is being seen by paediatric and child-therapy services. Keep that history with the nap-adjustment account; the mother is identified as a mother in these records.

Full passages: [sbu04-0306](#sbu04-0306) [sbu04-0307](#sbu04-0307) [sbu04-0308](#sbu04-0308).

### 3. No dedicated account of what a parent does after a device alarm.

Monitoring is covered, but an eleven-minute emergency journey, an app’s displayed age limit, a camera losing sight of a moving baby, and concern about whether kicks remain detectable are operational questions that reassurance/anxiety labels do not answer.

Full passages: [sbu04-0090](#sbu04-0090) [sbu04-0191](#sbu04-0191) [sbu18-0139](#sbu18-0139) [sbu18-0191](#sbu18-0191) [sbu18-0192](#sbu18-0192).

### 4. No separate treatment of parents asking for evidence about a philosophy of care.

Values on crying are covered. The request for research on gentle weaning versus self-weaning, aligned if possible with attachment/evolutionary parenting, asks for a kind of evidence and a moral frame as well as steps. The broad risk comparison that includes parental fear and deprivation also belongs here.

Full passages: [sbu14-0168](#sbu14-0168) [sbu14-0169](#sbu14-0169) [sbu14-0170](#sbu14-0170) [sbu18-0021](#sbu18-0021).

### 5. No dedicated product-incident/customer-service section.

Purchases fit §4.6 and money fits §8, but a child under a travel-crib mattress, a reported sagging play-yard mattress dismissed by customer service, and a gifted-crib fit decision involve different sources of trust and action. Keep these as attributed reports.

Full passages: [sbu14-0234](#sbu14-0234) [sbu14-0235](#sbu14-0235) [sbu18-0102](#sbu18-0102) [sbu18-0103](#sbu18-0103) [sbu14-0165](#sbu14-0165) [sbu14-0166](#sbu14-0166).

### 6. No clean division between sleep research and the rest of family life.

The dog whose barking disrupts sleep and whose owner describes wishing it would die, the cat shut outside a room after cosleeping begins, sibling-protecting screen purchases, creative work that remains impossible despite good child sleep, and an early-morning exercise routine blocked by a cosleeping toddler add household consequences to §9.1/§9.6.

Full passages: [sbu14-0195](#sbu14-0195) [sbu14-0196](#sbu14-0196) [sbu14-0106](#sbu14-0106) [sbu04-0033](#sbu04-0033) [sbu14-0387](#sbu14-0387) [sbu14-0388](#sbu14-0388) [sbu14-0413](#sbu14-0413).

### 7. Fragments need a provenance home, not an invented standalone interpretation.

Short fields preserved in full include a lead-in about dwelling on mistakes, an isolated mental-health clause, an unfinished PPD comparison and a partial childcare comment. Their supplied contexts support placement; the fragments cannot independently establish a new conclusion.

Full passages: [sbu04-0006](#sbu04-0006) [sbu04-0021](#sbu04-0021) [sbu04-0293](#sbu04-0293) [sbu04-0337](#sbu04-0337).

### 8. The generic article-linked passage needs an explicit evidence-class label.

The UR14 ledger identifies this generic article-linked post. Preserve it, but do not count it as independent testimony or prevalence. The same ledger says the father’s original text in another post was separated from chatbot advice and a rewrite; only that original field is included here.

Full passages: [sbu14-0381](#sbu14-0381) [sbu14-0115](#sbu14-0115).

## Money

The only currency-denominated amount in this share is **70€ for crib paint**, in [sbu14-0165](#sbu14-0165). It is not a sleep-plan price. The free four-night council programme is [sbu04-0258](#sbu04-0258). An app payment is explicit but unpriced in [sbu18-0249](#sbu18-0249). The purchased radar monitor has a contemplated return in [sbu04-0178](#sbu04-0178); no completed return, refund or money-back claim appears in this share. No quoted parent states willingness to pay a particular amount for a Mamivio-like document.

## Coverage and verification

Read in uninterrupted record-order passes: UR04 records 1–100, 101–200, 201–300 and 301–337; UR14 records 1–65, 66–155, 156–245, 246–330 and 331–413; UR18 records 1–110, 111–220, 221–325 and 326–371. These are row positions, not assumptions that ID suffixes are consecutive. No file or interval was skipped. All three ledger summaries were read, together with the README and the current findings’ structure, conclusions and later-wave qualifications.

Each reproduced voice passage was generated directly from its record’s `quote` field. The validator removes only Markdown’s added `> ` prefixes, then compares the resulting string exactly, without whitespace, punctuation or case normalisation, against that ID’s field. Current-conclusion excerpts are separately checked as exact substrings of the current findings. Every mentioned voice ID is checked against the union of the three assigned files. Unique primary assignments, file counts, complete coverage, currency references and direct-request IDs are also checked programmatically.

Coverage: **1,121 quotes in the share; 1,121 unique quotes reproduced verbatim (1,150 quotation blocks including repeats); 0 further support-only IDs; 0 IDs not mentioned at all; 0 unknown IDs; 0 verbatim mismatches.**
